Trace-Relating Compiler Correctness and Secure Compilation
Carmine Abate 1 Roberto Blanco 1 \cb Stefan Ciobâcă 2 Adrien Durier 1 Deepak Garg 3 Cătălin Hri \cb tcu 1 Marco Patrignani 4,5 Éric Tanter 6,1 Jérémy Thibault 1

Abstract
Compiler correctness is, in its simplest form, defined as the inclusion of the set of traces of the compiled program into the set of traces of the original program, which is equivalent to the preservation of all trace properties. Here traces collect, for instance, the externally observable events of each execution. This definition requires, however, the set of traces of the source and target languages to be exactly the same, which is not the case when the languages are far apart or when observations are fine-grained. To overcome this issue, we study a generalized compiler correctness definition, which uses source and target traces drawn from potentially different sets and connected by an arbitrary relation. We set out to understand what guarantees this generalized compiler correctness definition gives us when instantiated with a non-trivial relation on traces. When this trace relation is not equality, it is no longer possible to preserve the trace properties of the source program unchanged. Instead, we provide a generic characterization of the target trace property ensured by correctly compiling a program that satisfies a given source property, and dually, of the source trace property one is required to show in order to obtain a certain target property for the compiled code. We show that this view on compiler correctness can naturally account for undefined behavior, resource exhaustion, different source and target values, side-channels, and various abstraction mismatches. Finally, we show that the same generalization also applies to many secure compilation definitions, which characterize the protection of a compiled program against linked adversarial code.

Introduction

Compiler correctness is an old idea
[
38
,
41
,
42
]

that has seen a significant revival in recent times.
This new wave was started by the creation of the CompCert verified C
compiler
[
34
]
and continued by the proposal of many significant
extensions and variants of CompCert
[
61
,
43
,
63
,
31
,
30
,
8
,
67
,
23
,
56
,
12
,
9
]

and the success of many other milestone compiler verification projects,
including Vellvm
[
70
]
,
Pilsner
[
46
]
,
CakeML
[
64
]
,
CertiCoq
[
4
]
, etc. Yet, even for these verified compilers, the precise statement of
correctness matters.
Since proof assistants are used to conduct the verification, an external
observer does not have to understand the proofs in order to trust them, but one
still has to deeply understand the statement that was proved.
And this is true not just for correct compilation, but also for secure
compilation, which is the more recent idea that our compilation chains should
do more to also ensure security of our programs

[
26
,
3
]
.

Basic Compiler Correctness.

The gold standard for compiler correctness is
semantic preservation
, which
intuitively says that the semantics of a compiled program (in the target language)
is compatible with the semantics of the original program (in the source language).
For practical verified compilers, such as CompCert
[
34
]
and CakeML

[
64
]
, semantic preservation is stated extrinsically, by referring
to
traces
.
In these two settings,
a trace is an ordered sequence of events—such as inputs from and outputs to
an external environment—that are produced by the execution of a program.

A basic definition of compiler correctness can be given by the set inclusion of
the traces of the compiled program into the traces of the original program. Formally
[
34
]
:

Definition 1
(Basic Compiler Correctness (
\mathsf{CC}
))

A compiler
\downarrow
is
correct
iff

\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}~{}t.~{}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}}}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\bm{\rightsquigarrow}}}}t\Rightarrow\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\bm{\rightsquigarrow}}}}t.

This definition says that for any whole
1

1
For simplicity, for now
we ignore separate compilation and linking, returning to it in

§ 5
.

source program
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}
, if we compile it (denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}
), execute it with
respect to the semantics of the target language, and observe a trace

t
, then the original
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}
can produce
the same
trace
t
with
respect to the semantics of the source language.
2
2
2
Typesetting convention
[
50
]
: we use a
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{blue}}}
,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{sans\text{-}serif}}}
font for
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{source}}}
elements,
an
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{orange}}}
,
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{bold}}}
font for
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{target}}}
ones
and a
\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{black}}}
,
\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{italic}}}
font for elements common to both languages.

This definition is simple and easy to understand,
since it only references a few familiar concepts:
a compiler between a source and a target language,
each equipped with a trace-producing semantics (usually nondeterministic).

Beyond Basic Compiler Correctness.

This basic compiler correctness definition assumes that any trace produced by
a compiled program can be produced by the source program.
This is a very strict requirement, and in particular implies that the source and
target traces are drawn from the same set and that the same source trace
corresponds to a given target trace.
These assumptions are often too strong, and hence in practice verified compiler
efforts use different formulations of compiler correctness:

CompCert
[
34
]

The original compiler correctness theorem of CompCert
[
34
]
can be seen
as an instance of basic compiler correctness, but it does not provide any
guarantees for programs that can exhibit undefined
behavior
[
57
]
.
As allowed by the C standard, such unsafe programs are not even considered to be
in the source language, so are not quantified over.
This has important practical implications, since undefined behavior
often leads to exploitable security vulnerabilities
[
25
,
13
,
24
]
and serious confusion even among experienced C
and C++ developers
[
57
,
33
,
66
,
65
]
.
As such, since 2010, CompCert provides an additional top-level correctness
theorem
3
3
3
Stated at the top of the CompCert file

driver/Complements.v
and discussed by
Regehr
[
57
]
.

that better accounts for the presence of unsafe programs by
providing guarantees for them up to the point when they encounter
undefined behavior
[
57
]
.
This new theorem goes beyond the basic correctness definition above, as
a target trace need only correspond to a source trace
up to the
occurrence
of undefined behavior in the source trace.

CakeML
[
64
]

Compiler correctness for CakeML accounts for
memory exhaustion in target executions. Crucially, memory exhaustion events
cannot occur in source traces, only in target traces.
Hence, dually to CompCert, compiler correctness only requires source and
target traces to coincide up to the occurrence of a memory exhaustion event
in the target trace.

Trace-Relating Compiler Correctness.

Generalized formalizations of compiler correctness like the ones above can
be naturally expressed as instances of a uniform definition, which we call

trace-relating compiler correctness
.
This generalizes basic compiler correctness by
(a) considering that source and target traces belong to
possibly distinct

sets
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{Trace_{S}}}}
and
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{Trace_{T}}}}
, and (b) being parameterized
by an arbitrary
trace relation

\sim
.

Definition 2
(Trace-Relating Compiler Correctness
(
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
))

A compiler
\downarrow
is
correct
with respect to a trace relation

\sim\,\subseteq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{Trace_{S}}}}\times\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{Trace_{T}}}}
iff

\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}.\forall\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}.~{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\bm{\rightsquigarrow}}}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\Rightarrow\mathrel{\exists\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\sim\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}}\ldotp\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\bm{\rightsquigarrow}}}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}.

This definition requires that, for any target trace
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}
produced by the
compiled program
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}
, there exist a source trace
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}
that can be
produced by the original program
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}
and is
related
to
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}

according to
\sim
(i.e.,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\sim\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}
).
By choosing the trace relation appropriately, one can recover the different
notions of compiler correctness presented above:

Basic CC

Take
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\sim\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}
to be
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}=\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}
. Trivially, the basic CC of
1
is
\mathsf{CC^{=}}
.

CompCert

Undefined behavior is modeled in CompCert
as a trace-terminating event
\mathit{Goes\_wrong}
that can occur in any of its languages (source,
target, and all intermediate languages), so for a given
phase (or composition thereof), we have
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{Trace_{S}}}}=\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{Trace_{T}}}}
.
Nevertheless, the relation between source and target traces with which to
instantiate
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
to obtain CompCert’s current theorem is:

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\sim\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\quad\equiv\quad\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}=\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\vee(\exists m\leq\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}.~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}=m{\cdot}\mathit{Goes\_wrong}).

A compiler satisfying
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
for this trace relation
can turn a source trace ending in undefined behavior

m{\cdot}\mathit{Goes\_wrong}
(where “
{\cdot}
” is concatenation) either into
the same trace in the target (first disjunct), or into a target trace that
starts with the prefix
m
but then continues
arbitrarily

(second disjunct, “
{\leq}
” is the prefix relation).

CakeML

Here, target traces are sequences of symbols from an alphabet

\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\Sigma_{T}}}}
that has a specific trace-terminating event,

\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{Resource\_limit\_hit}}}
,
which is not available in the source alphabet
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\Sigma_{S}}}}

(i.e.,
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\Sigma_{T}}}}=\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\Sigma_{S}}}}\cup\{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{Resource\_limit\_hit}}}\}
.
Then, the compiler correctness theorem of CakeML can be obtained by
instantiating
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
with the following
\sim
relation:

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\sim\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\quad\equiv\quad\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}=\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\vee(\exists m.~{}m\leq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}.~{}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}=m{\cdot}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{Resource\_limit\_hit}}}).

The resulting
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
instance relates a target trace ending in

\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{Resource\_limit\_hit}}}
after executing
m
to a source trace that first
produces
m
and then continues in a way given by the semantics of the source
program.

Beyond undefined behavior and resource exhaustion, there are many other
practical uses for
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
: in this paper we show that it also
accounts for differences between source and target values, for a single
source output being turned into a series of target outputs, and for side-channels.

On the flip side, the compiler correctness statement and its implications can be
more difficult to understand for
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
than for
\mathsf{CC^{=}}
.
The full implications of choosing a particular
\sim
relation can be subtle.
In fact, using a bad relation can make the compiler correctness statement trivial or unexpected.
For instance, it should be easy to see that if one uses the total relation, which
relates all
source traces to all target ones, the
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
property holds for every
compiler, yet it might take one a bit more effort to understand that the
same is true even for the following relation:

\displaystyle\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\sim\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\quad\equiv\quad\exists\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\bm{\rightsquigarrow}}}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\bm{\rightsquigarrow}}}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}.

Reasoning About Trace Properties.

To understand more about a particular
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
instance, we propose to also
look at how it preserves
trace properties
—defined as sets of allowed
traces
[
32
]
—from the source to the target.
For instance, it is well known that
\mathsf{CC^{=}}
is equivalent to the
preservation of all trace properties (where
W\models\pi
reads
“
W
satisfies
\pi
” and stands for

\forall t.~{}W{\bm{\rightsquigarrow}}t\Rightarrow t\in\pi
):

\mathsf{CC^{=}}\quad\equiv\quad\forall\pi\in 2^{\text{Trace}}~{}\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}.~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\models}}}\pi\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\models}}}\pi.

However, to the best of our knowledge, similar results have not been formulated for
trace relations beyond equality, when it is no longer possible to preserve
the trace properties of the source program unchanged.
For trace-relating compiler correctness, where source and target traces can be
drawn from different sets and related by an arbitrary trace relation, there are
two crucial questions to ask:

1.

For a source trace property
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}
of a program—established for
instance by formal verification—what is the strongest
target property that any
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
compiler is guaranteed to ensure for
the produced target program?

2.

For a target trace property
{\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}}
, what is the weakest source
property we need to show of the original source program to obtain

\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
for the result of any
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
compiler?

Far from being mere hypothetical questions, they can help the developer of a
verified compiler to better understand the compiler correctness theorem they
are proving, and we expect that any user of such a compiler will need to ask
either one or the other if they are to make use of that theorem.
In this work we provide a simple and natural answer to these
questions, for any instance of
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
. Building upon a bijection
between relations and
Galois connections
[
20
,
5
,
44
]
,
we observe that any trace relation
\sim
corresponds to two
property
mappings

\tilde{\tau}
and
\tilde{\sigma}
, which are functions
mapping source properties to target ones (
\tilde{\tau}
standing for
“to target”) and target properties to source ones (
\tilde{\sigma}

standing for “to source”):

\tilde{\tau}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}})=\left\{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\,\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\,\exists\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\ldotp\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\sim\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\wedge\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}\right\};\qquad\tilde{\sigma}(\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}})=\left\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\,\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\,\forall\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\ldotp\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\sim\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\Rightarrow\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\in\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}\right\}.

The
existential image
of
\sim
,
\tilde{\tau}
, answers the
first question above by mapping a given source property
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}
to
the target property that contains all target traces for which
there exists a related source trace
that satisfies
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}
.
Dually, the
universal image
of
\sim
,
\tilde{\sigma}
,
answers the second question by mapping a given target property

\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
to the source property that contains all source traces for
which
all related target traces
satisfy
\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
.
We introduce two new correct compilation definitions in terms of
trace property preservation
(
\mathsf{TP}
):

\mathsf{TP^{\tilde{\tau}}}
quantifies over all source trace properties
and uses
\tilde{\tau}
to obtain the corresponding target properties.

\mathsf{TP}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
quantifies over all target trace
properties and uses
\tilde{\sigma}
to obtain the corresponding source
properties.
We prove that these two definitions are equivalent to
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
,
yielding a novel trinitarian view of compiler correctness (
Figure 1
).

\sim

\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
\mathsf{TP^{\tilde{\tau}}}
\mathsf{TP^{\tilde{\sigma}}}
\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}.~{}\forall\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}.~{}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\bm{\rightsquigarrow}}}}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\Rightarrow\mathrel{\exists\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\sim\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}}\ldotp\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\bm{\rightsquigarrow}}}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}

\begin{aligned} &\forall\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}.~{}\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}.~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\models}}}~{}\tilde{\sigma}(\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}})\\
&\ \Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}~{}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\models}}}~{}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}\end{aligned}\equiv

\equiv\begin{aligned} &\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}.~{}\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}.~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\models}}}~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}\\
&\ \Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}~{}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\models}}}~{}\tilde{\tau}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}})\end{aligned}

\equiv

Figure 1
:
The equivalent compiler correctness definitions forming our trinitarian view.

Contributions.

\blacktriangleright

We propose a new trinitarian view of compiler correctness that
accounts for non-trivial trace relations.
While, as discussed above, specific instances of the
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
definition
have already been used in practice,
we seem to be the first to propose assessing the
meaningfulness of
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
instances in terms of how properties are
preserved between the source and the target, and in particular by looking at the
property mappings
\tilde{\sigma}
and
\tilde{\tau}
induced by the trace
relation
\sim
.
We prove that
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
,
\mathsf{TP^{\tilde{\sigma}}}
, and

\mathsf{TP^{\tilde{\tau}}}
are equivalent for any trace relation (
§ 2.2
),
as illustrated in
Figure 1
.
In the opposite direction, we show that for every trace relation
corresponding to a given Galois connection

[
20
]
, an analogous equivalence holds.
Finally, we extend these results (
§ 2.3
)
from the preservation of trace properties to
the larger class of subset-closed hyperproperties (e.g., noninterference).

\blacktriangleright

We use
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
compilers of various complexities
to illustrate that our view on compiler
correctness naturally accounts
for undefined behavior (
§ 3.1
),
resource exhaustion (
§ 3.2
),
different source and target values (
§ 3.3
),
and differences in the granularity of data and observable events
(
§ 3.4
).
We expect these ideas to apply to any other discrepancies between source
and target traces.
For each compiler we show how to choose the relation
between source and target traces and how the induced property mappings preserve
interesting trace properties and subset-closed hyperproperties.
We look at the way particular
\tilde{\sigma}
and
\tilde{\tau}
work on
different kinds of properties and how the produced properties can be expressed for
different kinds of traces.

\blacktriangleright

We analyze the impact of correct compilation on
noninterference
[
22
]
, showing what can still be preserved
(and thus also what is lost)
when target observations are finer than source ones, e.g., side-channel
observations (
§ 4
). We formalize
the guarantee obtained by correct compilation of a noninterfering
program as
abstract
noninterference

[
21
]
, a weakening of
target noninterference. Dually, we identify a family of
declassifications of target noninterference for which source
reasoning is possible.

\blacktriangleright

Finally, we show that the trinitarian view also extends to a
large class of
secure compilation
definitions
[
2
]
,
formally characterizing the protection of the compiled program against linked
adversarial code (
§ 5
).
For each secure compilation definition we again
propose both a property-free characterization in the style of
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
,
and two characterizations in terms of preserving a class of source or target
properties satisfied against arbitrary adversarial contexts.
The additional quantification over contexts allows for finer
distinctions when considering different property classes, so we
study mapping classes not only of trace properties and hyperproperties,
but also of relational hyperproperties
[
2
]
.
An example secure compiler accounting for a target that can produce additional
trace events that are not possible in the source illustrates this approach.

The paper closes with discussions of
related (
§ 6
) and future work (
§ 7
).
The appendix contains omitted technical details.

The traces considered in our examples are structured, usually as sequences of
events.
We notice however that unless explicitly mentioned, all our definitions and
results are more general and make no assumption whatsoever about the structure
of traces.
Most of the theorems formally or informally mentioned in the paper
were mechanized in the Coq proof assistant and are marked with

.
This development has around 10k
lines of code, is described in the online appendix, and is available
at the following address:

https://github.com/secure-compilation/different_traces
.

2
Trace-Relating Compiler Correctness

In this section, we start by generalizing the trace property preservation
definitions at the end of the introduction to
\mathsf{TP^{\sigma}}
and

\mathsf{TP^{\tau}}
, which depend on two
arbitrary
mappings

\sigma
and
\tau
(
§ 2.1
).
We prove that, whenever
\sigma
and
\tau
form a Galois connection,

\mathsf{TP^{\sigma}}
and
\mathsf{TP^{\tau}}
are equivalent
(
Theorem 2.1
).
We then exploit a bijective correspondence between trace relations and Galois
connections to close the trinitarian view (
§ 2.2
),
with two main benefits:
first, it helps us assess the meaningfulness of a given trace relation
by looking at the property mappings it induces; second, it allows
us to construct new compiler correctness definitions starting from a
desired mapping of properties.
Finally, we generalize the classic result that compiler correctness (i.e.,
\mathsf{CC^{=}}
) is enough to preserve not just trace properties but also all
subset-closed hyperproperties
[
14
]
.
For this, we show that
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
is also equivalent to subset-closed
hyperproperty preservation, for which we also define both a version in
terms of
\tilde{\sigma}
and a version in terms of
\tilde{\tau}

(
§ 2.3
).

2.1
Property Mappings

As explained in
§ 1
, trace-relating compiler correctness

\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
, by itself, lacks a crisp description of which trace properties
are preserved by compilation.
Since even the syntax of traces can differ between source and target, one can
either look at trace properties of the source (but then one needs to interpret
them in the target), or at trace properties of the target (but then one needs to
interpret them in the source).
Formally we need two property mappings,
\tau:2^{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{{Trace}_{S}}}}}\to 2^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{{Trace}_{T}}}}}
and

~{}\sigma:2^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{{Trace}_{T}}}}}\to 2^{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{{Trace}_{S}}}}}
, which lead us to the following
generalization of trace property preservation (
\mathsf{TP}
).

Definition 3
(
\mathsf{TP^{\sigma}}
and
\mathsf{TP^{\tau}}
)

Given two property mappings,
\tau:2^{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{{Trace}_{S}}}}}\rightarrow 2^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{Trace_{T}}}}}
and

\sigma:2^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{{Trace}_{T}}}}}\rightarrow 2^{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{{Trace}_{S}}}}}
, for a compilation chain
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\cdot}}}\right\downarrow}}
we define:

\mathsf{TP^{\tau}}\equiv\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}.~{}\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}.~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\models}}}~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}~{}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\models}}}~{}\tau(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}});\;\;\;\mathsf{TP^{\sigma}}\equiv\forall\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}.~{}\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}.~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\models}}}~{}\sigma(\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}})\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}~{}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\models}}}~{}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}.

For an arbitrary source program
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}
,
\tau
interprets a source
property
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}
as the
target guarantee
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}
.
Dually,
\sigma
defines a
source obligation
sufficient for the
satisfaction of a target property
\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
after
compilation. Ideally:

•

Given
\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
, the target interpretation of
the source obligation
\sigma(\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}})
should actually guarantee
that
\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
holds,
i.e.,
\tau(\sigma(\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}))\subseteq\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
;

•

Dually for
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}
, we would not want the source obligation for

\tau(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}})
to be harder than
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}
itself,
i.e.,
\sigma(\tau(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}))\supseteq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}
.

These requirements are satisfied when the two maps form a
Galois
connection
between the posets of source and target properties
ordered by inclusion.
We briefly recall the definition and the characteristic property of
Galois connections
[
16
,
39
]
.

Definition 4
(Galois connection)

Let
(X,\preceq)
and

(Y,\sqsubseteq)
be two posets. A pair of maps,

\alpha:X\to Y
,
\gamma:Y\to X
is a Galois connection
\mathit{iff}

it satisfies the
adjunction law
:

\forall x\in X.~{}\forall y\in Y.~{}\alpha(x)\sqsubseteq y\iff x\preceq\gamma(y)
.

\alpha
(resp.
\gamma
)
is the lower (upper) adjoint or
abstraction (concretization) function and
Y
(
X
)
the abstract (concrete) domain.

We will often write

\alpha:(X,\preceq)\leftrightarrows(Y,\sqsubseteq):\gamma
to
denote a Galois connection, or simply

\alpha:X\leftrightarrows Y:\gamma
, or even

\alpha\leftrightarrows\gamma
when the involved posets are clear
from context.

Lemma 1
(Characteristic property of Galois connections)

If
\alpha{:}(X,\preceq)\leftrightarrows{(Y,\sqsubseteq){:}\gamma}
is a Galois connection, then
\alpha,\gamma
are monotone and
they satisfy these properties:

\displaystyle i)

\displaystyle\quad\forall x\in X.~{}x\preceq\gamma(\alpha(x));

\displaystyle ii)

\displaystyle\quad\forall y\in Y.~{}\alpha(\gamma(y))\sqsubseteq y.

If
X,Y
are complete lattices, then
\alpha
is continuous,
i.e.,
\forall F\subseteq X.~{}\alpha(\bigsqcup F)=\bigsqcup\alpha(F)
.

If two property mappings,
\tau
and
\sigma
, form a Galois connection on trace
properties ordered by set inclusion,
1
(with

\alpha=\tau
and
\gamma=\sigma
) tells us that they
satisfy the ideal conditions we discussed above, i.e.,
\tau(\sigma(\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}))\subseteq\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}
and

\sigma(\tau(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}))\supseteq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}
.
4
4
4
While
target traces are often
“more concrete”
than source ones,
trace properties
2^{\text{Trace}}
(which in Coq we represent as
the function type
\text{Trace}{\to}\text{Prop}
)
are contravariant in
Trace
and thus
target properties correspond to the
abstract domain
.

The two ideal conditions on
\tau
and
\sigma
are sufficient to
show the equivalence of the criteria they define, respectively

\mathsf{TP^{\tau}}
and
\mathsf{TP^{\sigma}}
.

Theorem 2.1
(
\mathsf{TP^{\tau}}
and
\mathsf{TP^{\sigma}}
coincide

)

Let
\tau:2^{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{Trace_{S}}}}}\rightleftarrows 2^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{{Trace}_{T}}}}}:\sigma
be a
Galois connection, with
\tau
and
\sigma
the lower and upper
adjoints (resp.). Then
\mathsf{TP^{\tau}}\iff\mathsf{TP^{\sigma}}
.

2.2
Trace Relations and Property Mappings

We now investigate the relation between
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
,

\mathsf{TP^{\tau}}
and
\mathsf{TP^{\sigma}}
.
We show that for a trace relation and its corresponding Galois
connection (
2
), the three criteria are
equivalent (
Theorem 2.3
).
This equivalence offers interesting insights for both verification and
design of a correct compiler. For a
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
compiler, the
equivalence makes explicit both the guarantees one has after
compilation (
\tilde{\tau}
) and source proof obligations to ensure
the satisfaction of a given target property (
\tilde{\sigma}
). On
the other hand, a compiler designer might first determine the target
guarantees the compiler itself must provide, i.e.,
\tau
, and then
prove an equivalent statement,
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
, for which more convenient
proof techniques exist in the literature
[
7
,
64
]
.

Definition 5
(Existential and Universal Image
[
20
]
)

Given any two sets
X
and
Y
and a relation
{\sim}\subseteq A\times B
,
define its existential or direct image,
\tilde{\tau}:2^{X}\to 2^{Y}
and
its universal image,
\tilde{\sigma}:2^{Y}\to 2^{X}
as follows:

\tilde{\tau}=\lambda~{}\pi\in 2^{X}.~{}\left\{y\,\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\,\exists x\ldotp x\sim y\wedge x\in\pi\right\};\tilde{\sigma}=\lambda~{}\pi\in 2^{Y}.~{}\left\{x\,\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\,\forall y\ldotp x\sim y\Rightarrow y\in\pi\right\}.

When trace relations are considered, the existential and
universal images can be used to instantiate
3
leading
to the trinitarian view already mentioned in
§ 1
.

Theorem 2.2
(Trinitarian View

)

For any trace relation
\sim
and its existential and universal images

\tilde{\tau}
and
\tilde{\sigma}
, we have:

\mathsf{TP^{\tilde{\tau}}}\iff\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}\iff\mathsf{TP^{\tilde{\sigma}}}
.

This result relies both on
Theorem 2.1
and on the fact that
the existential and universal images of a trace relation form a Galois
connection (

). Below we further generalize this result
(
Theorem 2.3
) relying on a bijective
correspondence between trace relations and Galois connections on
properties.

Lemma 2
(Trace relations
\cong
Galois connections on trace properties)

The function

\sim\;\mapsto\tilde{\tau}\leftrightarrows\tilde{\sigma}
that
maps a trace relation to its existential and universal images is a
bijection between trace relations

2^{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{Trace_{S}}}}\times\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{Trace_{T}}}}}
and Galois connections on
trace properties
2^{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{{Trace}_{S}}}}}\leftrightarrows 2^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{{Trace}_{T}}}}}
.
Its inverse is
\tau\leftrightarrows\sigma\mapsto\hat{\sim}
,
where

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\,\hat{\sim}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\equiv\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\in\tau(\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\})
.

Proof

Gardiner et al.
[
20
]
show that the existential image is a
functor from the category of sets and relations to the category of
predicate transformers, mapping a set
X\mapsto 2^{X}
and a relation

\sim\;\subseteq X\times Y\mapsto\tilde{\tau}:2^{X}\to 2^{Y}
.
They also show that such a functor is an isomorphism – hence
bijective – when one considers only monotonic predicate
transformers that
have a – unique – upper adjoint.
The universal image of
\sim
,
\tilde{\sigma}
, is the unique
adjoint of
\tilde{\tau}
(

), hence

\sim\;\mapsto\tilde{\tau}\leftrightarrows\tilde{\sigma}
is
itself bijective. ∎

The bijection just introduced allows us to generalize

Theorem 2.2
and switch between the three views of
compiler correctness described earlier at will.

Theorem 2.3
(Correspondence of Criteria)

For any trace relation
\sim
and corresponding Galois connection

\tau\leftrightarrows\sigma
, we have:

\mathsf{TP^{\tau}}\iff\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}\iff\mathsf{TP^{\sigma}}
.

Proof

For a trace relation
\sim
and the Galois connection

\tilde{\tau}\leftrightarrows\tilde{\sigma}
, the result follows
from
Theorem 2.2
.
For a Galois connection
\tau\leftrightarrows\sigma
and

\hat{\sim}
, use
2
to conclude that the
existential and universal images of
\hat{\sim}
coincide with
\tau

and
\sigma
, respectively; the goal then follows from
Theorem 2.2
.
∎

We conclude by explicitly noting that sometimes the lifted properties may be
trivial: the target guarantee can be the true property (the set of all traces),
or the source obligation the false property (the empty set of traces).
This might be the case when source observations abstract away too much
information (
§ 3.2
presents an example).

2.3
Preservation of Subset-Closed Hyperproperties

A
\mathsf{CC^{=}}
compiler ensures the preservation not only of trace
properties, but also of all subset-closed hyperproperties, which are known
to be preserved by refinement
[
14
]
.
An example of a subset-closed hyperproperty is
noninterference

[
14
]
; a
\mathsf{CC^{=}}
compiler thus guarantees that if
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}
is
noninterfering with respect to the inputs and outputs in the trace then so is

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}
.
To be able to talk about how (hyper)properties such as noninterference
are preserved, in this section we propose another trinitarian view involving

\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
and preservation of subset-closed hyperproperties
(
Theorem 2.4
),
slightly weakened in that source and target property mappings will need to be
closed under subsets.

First, recall that a program satisfies a hyperproperty when its complete
set of traces, which from now on we will call its
behavior
, is a member of
the hyperproperty
[
14
]
.

Definition 6
(Hyperproperty Satisfaction)

A program
W
satisfies a hyperproperty
H
, written

W\models H
,
iff
\mathit{beh}(W)\in H
, where

\mathit{beh}(W)=\left\{t\,\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\,W{\rightsquigarrow}t\right\}
.

Hyperproperty preservation is a strong requirement in general.
Fortunately, many interesting hyperproperties are
subset-closed
(
\mathit{SCH}

for short), which simplifies their preservation
since it suffices to show that the behaviors of the compiled program refine the
behaviors of the source one, which coincides with the statement of
\mathsf{CC^{=}}
.

To talk about hyperproperty preservation in the trace-relating setting, we need
an interpretation of source hyperproperties into the target and vice versa.
The one we consider builds on top of the two trace property mappings
\tau
and

\sigma
, which are naturally lifted to hyperproperty mappings.
This way we are able to extract two hyperproperty mappings from a trace relation
similarly to
§ 2.2
:

Definition 7
(Lifting property mappings to hyperproperty mappings)

Let
\tau:2^{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{{Trace}_{S}}}}}\to 2^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{{Trace}_{T}}}}}
and
\sigma:2^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{{Trace}_{T}}}}}\to 2^{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{{Trace}_{S}}}}}
be arbitrary property mappings.
The images of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{H_{S}}}}\in 2^{2^{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{{Trace}_{S}}}}}},\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{H_{T}}}}\in 2^{2^{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{{Trace}_{T}}}}}}
under
\tau
and
\sigma
are, respectively:

\displaystyle\tau(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{H_{S}}}})

\displaystyle=\left\{\tau(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}})\,\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{H_{S}}}}\right\};

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{H_{T}}}})

\displaystyle=\left\{\sigma(\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}})\,\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\,\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}\in\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{H_{T}}}}\right\}.

Formally we are defining two new mappings, this time on
hyperproperties, but by a small abuse of notation we still denote them
by
\tau
and
\sigma
.

Interestingly, it is not possible to apply the argument used for
\mathsf{CC^{=}}

to show that a
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
compiler guarantees

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}~{}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\models}}}~{}\tilde{\tau}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{H_{S}}}})
whenever
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\models}}}~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{H_{S}}}}
.
This is in fact not true because direct images do not necessarily
preserve subset-closure
[
37
,
45
]
.
To fix this we close the image of
\tilde{\tau}

and
\tilde{\sigma}
under subsets (denoted as
\mathit{Cl_{\subseteq}}
)
and obtain:

Theorem 2.4
(Preservation of Subset-Closed Hyperproperties

)

For any trace relation

\sim
and its existential and
universal images lifted to hyperproperties,
\tilde{\tau}
and
\tilde{\sigma}
, and for

\mathit{Cl}_{\subseteq}(H)=\{\pi\mid\exists\pi^{\prime}\in H.~{}\pi\subseteq\pi^{\prime}\}
,
we have:

\mathsf{SCHP^{\mathit{Cl}_{\subseteq}\circ\tilde{\tau}}}\iff\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}\iff\mathsf{SCHP^{\mathit{Cl}_{\subseteq}\circ\tilde{\sigma}}},\text{ where}

\displaystyle\mathsf{SCHP^{\mathit{Cl}_{\subseteq}\circ\tilde{\tau}}}

\displaystyle\equiv\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{H_{S}}}}\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{SCH_{S}}}}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\models}}}~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{H_{S}}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}~{}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\models}}}~{}\mathit{Cl}_{\subseteq}(\tilde{\tau}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{H_{S}}}}));

\displaystyle\mathsf{SCHP^{\mathit{Cl}_{\subseteq}\circ\tilde{\sigma}}}

\displaystyle\equiv\forall\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\forall\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{H_{T}}}}\in\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{SCH_{T}}}}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\models}}}~{}\mathit{Cl}_{\subseteq}(\tilde{\sigma}(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{H_{T}}}}))\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@gray@stroke{0}\pgfsys@color@gray@fill{0}{\left.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{W}}}\right\downarrow}}~{}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\models}}}~{}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{H_{T}}}}.

Theorem 2.4
makes us aware of the potential loss of precision when
interested in preserving subset-closed hyperproperties through compilation.
In
§ 4
we focus on a security
relevant subset-closed hyperproperty, noninterference, and show that
such a loss of precision can be intended as a declassification of
noninterference.

3
Instances of Trace-Relating Compiler Correctness

The trace-relating view of compiler correctness above can serve as a unifying
framework for studying a range of interesting compilers.
This section provides several representative instantiations of the framework:
source languages with undefined behavior that compilation can turn into
arbitrary target behavior (
§ 3.1
),
target languages with resource exhaustion that cannot
happen in the source (
§ 3.2
),
changes in the representation of values (
§ 3.3
),
and differences in the granularity of data and observable events
(
§ 3.4
).

3.1
Undefined Behavior

We start by expanding upon the discussion of undefined behavior
in
§ 1
.
We first study the model of CompCert, where source and target alphabets are the same,
including the event for undefined behavior.
The trace relation weakens equality by allowing undefined behavior to be
replaced with an arbitrary sequence of events.

Example 1
(CompCert-like Undefined Behavior Relation)

Source and target traces are sequences of events drawn from
\Sigma
,
where
\mathit{Goes\_wrong}\in\Sigma
is a terminal event that represents an undefined
behavior.
We then use the trace relation from the introduction:

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\sim\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\quad\equiv\quad\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}=\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\vee\exists m\leq\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}.~{}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}={m}\cdot\mathit{Goes\_wrong}.

Each trace of a target program produced by a
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
compiler is either also a
trace of the original source program or it has a finite prefix that the source
program also produces, immediately before encountering undefined behavior.
As explained in
§ 1
, one of the correctness theorems in CompCert
can be rephrased as this variant of
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
.

We proved that the property mappings induced by the relation can be written as (

):

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}(\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}})=

\displaystyle\ \left\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\,\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}{\in}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}\wedge\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{s}}}\neq m{\cdot}\mathit{Goes\_wrong}\right\}\cup\left\{{m}{\cdot}\mathit{Goes\_wrong}\,\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\,\forall\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}.~{}m{\leq}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}{\implies}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}{\in}\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{\mathrm{T}}}}}\right\};

\displaystyle\tilde{\tau}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}})=

\displaystyle\ \left\{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\,\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}{\in}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}\right\}\cup\left\{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}\,\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\,\exists m\leq\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{t}}}.~{}{m}{\cdot}\mathit{Goes\_wrong}\in\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}\right\}.

These two mappings explain what a
\mathsf{CC^{\sim}}
compiler ensures
for the
\sim
relation above.
The target-to-source mapping
\tilde{\sigma}
states that to prove that
a compiled program has a property
\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{T}}}}
using source-level reasoning,
one has to prove that any trace produced by the source program must either be a target
trace satisfying
\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{T}}}}
or have undefined behavior, but only provided that

any continuation
of the trace
substituted for the undefined behavior satisfies
\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.23,0.13}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{0}{0.77}{0.87}{0}{\pi_{T}}}}
.
The source-to-target mapping
\tilde{\tau}
states that by compiling a
program satisfying a property
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0.5,1}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@stroke{1}{0.50}{0}{0}\pgfsys@color@cmyk@fill{1}{0.50}{0}{0}{\pi_{S}}}}
we obtain a program that
produces traces that satisfy the same property or that extend a source
trace that ends in undefined behavior.

These definitions can help us reason about programs. For instance,

\tilde{\sigma}
specifies that, to prove that an event does not happen in the
target, it is not enough to prove that it does not happen in the source: it is
also necessary to prove that the source program is does not have any undefined behavior (second disjunct).
Indeed, if it had an undefined behavior, its continuations could exhibit
the unwanted event.

\boxdot

This relation can be easily generalized to other settings.
For instance, consider the setting in which we compile down to a low-level language like
machine code.
Target traces can now contain new events that cannot occur in the source: indeed,
in modern architectures like x86 a compiler typically uses only a fraction of
the available instruction set.
Some instructions might even perform dangerous operations, such as writing to the
hard drive.
Formally, the source and target do not have the same events any more.
Thus, we consider a source alphabet
